<document filename="MPC-overview.md"> # From PID to Predictive: A Hands-On Guide to Model Predictive Control in the Era of Industrial Digital Transformation <h2>Introduction: The Importance of MPC and Machine Learning Strategies</h2> <p>Industrial process control is undergoing a seismic shift as digital transformation and Industry 4.0+ bring information technology (IT) and operational technology (OT) closer together. Full convergence of these technologies is necessary to realize world-class optimization in manufacturing. This requires innovative solutions to address complex infrastructure (technical debt) amid pressures like greater product variety, shorter production runs, higher raw material utilization, stricter quality standards, sustainability goals, and environmental requirements—all while maximizing efficiency and uptime.</p> <p>Traditional control methods like PID can only take a facility so far. Machine learning (ML) and model predictive control (MPC) emerge as game-changers. These concepts are not new, but accessible tools now drive innovation.</p> <p>With ML and MPC acting as intelligent layers on existing automation systems, continuously optimizing multiple objectives (cost reduction, quality improvement) in real time for unprecedented efficiency. Advanced process control (APC) strategies like MPC are key enablers for Industry 4.0+—core capabilities for digital transformation. By leveraging data and models, they help industries remain competitive in connected, data-driven operations.</p> <h3>Foundations and Motivation</h3> <p>Traditional PID control provides stable regulation for single loops but struggles with multivariable interactions, delays, and constraints common in modern manufacturing. PID reacts to current errors without prediction, leading to suboptimal performance under disturbances (e.g., varying mash density affecting proofing).</p> <p>The industry pivots to model-based strategies like MPC, which use dynamic models to forecast behavior and optimize actions. In the Big Data era of Industry 4.0, ML enhances this by learning from vast datasets, enabling adaptive, proactive control. This convergence of PID fundamentals with MPC/ML hybrids bridges gaps, offering resilience and efficiency for sustainability and agility goals.</p> <h3>Modeling and MPC Design</h3> <p>Develop dynamic models via first principles (e.g., mass/energy balances) or data-driven methods (e.g., subspace ID). These models form MPC's core, predicting future states to minimize costs subject to constraints.</p> <p><strong>First-Principles Example</strong> (Heat Exchanger): <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math class="mopen mtight">(</span><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">T</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1645em;"><span style="top:-2.357em;margin-left:-0.1389em;margin-right:0.0714em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.5em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size3 size1 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">c</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" class="mclose mtight">)</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.5423em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose nulldelimiter"></span></span></span></span></span> Variables: T (temperature °F), t (time s), F (flow gpm), T_in (inlet °F), UA (heat transfer BTU/°F-s), T_c (coolant °F), V (volume gal), ρ (density lbs/gal), C_p (heat capacity BTU/lb-°F).</p> <p><strong>Python (SciPy for Simulation)</strong>:</p> <pre><code class="language-python">from scipy.integrate import odeint import numpy as np def model(T, t, F, T_in, UA, T_c, V, rho, Cp): dTdt = (F * (T_in - T) + UA * (T_c - T)) / (V * rho * Cp) return dTdt params = (100, 200, 50, 100, 10, 8.345, 1.0) # Example values t = np.linspace(0, 100, 100) T0 = 150 T_sim = odeint(model, T0, t, args=params) </code></pre> <p>For MPC design, discretize and formulate QP (see example below).</p> <h3>Integration with PLC Control</h3> <p>Integrate MPC with PLCs by linking outputs to PIDE setpoints. Use OPC UA for data exchange; handle mode switching (e.g., from MPC to manual/PID) with bumpless transfer (smooth handover to avoid jumps). Ensure safety via watchdogs (fallback to PID on MPC failure).</p> <p><strong>Python (control for PID-MPC Hybrid)</strong>:</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import control import numpy as np # PID model Kp, Ti, Td = 1.0, 2.0, 0.5 pid = control.tf([Kp * Td, Kp, Kp / Ti], [1, 0]) # Approx form # MPC setpoint from simulation (placeholder) sp_mpc = np.sin(np.linspace(0, 10, 100)) * 50 + 150 # Simulate PID tracking MPC SP plant = control.tf(1, [2, 1]) # Example process closed = control.feedback(pid * plant) t, y = control.step_response(closed * control.tf(sp_mpc, 1), T=np.linspace(0, 10, 100)) # Plot y vs sp_mpc for handover check </code></pre> <p>For plc-gbt, use Python wrappers to read PLC tags and write MPC outputs.</p> <h3>Advanced Enhancements</h3> <p>Enhance control with adaptive ML like NARX for nonlinear modeling. Case study: NARX improves accuracy in distillation condensers by capturing delays/disturbances, serving as soft sensor for quality (e.g., proof prediction).</p> <p><strong>NARX Case Study</strong> (Python with PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import torch import torch.nn as nn import numpy as np class NARX(nn.Module): def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim): super().__init__() self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(in_dim, 64), nn.ReLU(), nn.Linear(64, out_dim)) def forward(self, x): return self.net(x) # Train on condenser data (u: flow/reflux, y: temp/concentration) model = NARX(4, 2) # e.g., 2 lagged u/y optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters()) # Training loop (as in guide) # Use as soft sensor: predict y from u, feed to MPC </code></pre> <p>Incorporate for adaptive control: Retrain NARX on plc-gbt data for evolving processes.</p> <h3>Best Practices and Troubleshooting</h3> <p>Tune MPC/PID via simulation (e.g., FIT >80%). Maintain fidelity with periodic re-identification. Diagnose issues: Monitor residuals (whiteness test), use plc-gbt for metrics (e.g., ISE). Fallbacks: Switch to PID on high variance. Best practices: Start small (single loop), scale to multivariable; validate hybrids offline.</p> <p><strong>Troubleshooting Table</strong>:</p> <table><thead><tr><th>Issue</th><th>Cause</th><th>Fix</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Oscillations</td><td>Model mismatch</td><td>Retune λ or retrain model</td></tr><tr><td>Constraint Violations</td><td>Unmodeled DVs</td><td>Add slack variables in QP</td></tr><tr><td>Slow Response</td><td>Conservative λ</td><td>Reduce λ, test stability</td></tr><tr><td>Overfitting</td><td>High model order</td><td>Truncate SVD (energy >95%)</td></tr></tbody></table> <h2>Algorithms for Process Control</h2> <p>Each entry includes "When to Use," equations, variables, and Python examples for hands-on learning.</p> <h3>1. AutoRegressive with eXogenous Inputs (ARX / ARMAX)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Simple linear processes with known inputs/outputs: Quick baselines in stable lines.</li> <li>Fast online identification: Track drifts via recursive least squares.</li> <li>Low computation: Suitable for PLCs.</li> <li>Noise modeling (ARMAX): Separate dynamics from fluctuations.</li> <li>Foundation for predictive control: Anticipates outputs.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: ARX: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> y(k) + \sum_{i=1}^{n_a} a_i y(k-i) = \sum_{j=1}^{n_b} b_j u(k-j+1) + e(k) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1.0085em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.03588em;">q</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8141em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">−</span><span class="mord mtight">1</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: Backward-shift operator.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoregressive_exogenous_model">Autoregressive exogenous model</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (control &#x26; NumPy):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np import control # Simulate data (replace with real) n_a, n_b = 2, 2 a = [0.5, -0.3] b = [1.0, 0.5] u = np.random.rand(100) y = np.zeros(100) for k in range(max(n_a, n_b), 100): y[k] = -sum(a[i-1] * y[k-i] for i in range(1, n_a+1)) + sum(b[j-1] * u[k-j] for j in range(1, n_b+1)) + np.random.normal(0, 0.1) # Estimate ARX sys_arx = control.arx(np.vstack([u, y]).T, n_a, n_b) print("Estimated coefficients:", sys_arx.a, sys_arx.b) </code></pre> <h3>2. Subspace Identification (N4SID, MOESP)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Multivariable systems with interactions.</li> <li>Black-box modeling without priors.</li> <li>Noise robustness via SVD projection.</li> <li>Efficient for large data.</li> <li>Foundation for MPC/observers.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>A</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo separator="true">,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>D</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> x_{k+1} = A x_k + B u_k, \quad y_k = C x_k + D u_k </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6389em;vertical-align:-0.2083em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3361em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span><span class="mbin mtight">+</span><span class="mord mtight">1</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2083em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8333em;vertical-align:-0.15em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal">A</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3361em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2222em;"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2222em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8778em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.05017em;">B</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">u</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3361em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subspace_identification">Subspace identification</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (control):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np import control # Simulate data (replace with real) n, l, m = 4, 2, 2 sys = control.rss(n, l, m, tsamp=0.1) u = np.random.rand(100, m) y = control.forced_response(sys, U=u.T)[1].T data = control.iddata(y, u, tsamp=0.1) model_order = 4 sys_n4sid = control.n4sid(data, model_order) print("A matrix:", sys_n4sid.A) </code></pre> <h3>3. Eigensystem Realization Algorithm (ERA)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Reduced-order models from impulse/step data.</li> <li>Vibration-heavy manufacturing.</li> <li>Learning dominant patterns for predictive MPC.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{i \times r} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi>r</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> V_r </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8333em;vertical-align:-0.15em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.02778em;">r</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: Truncated right vectors, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{j \times r} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8247em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.05724em;">j</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.02778em;">r</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> H(1) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.08125em;">H</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">)</span></span></span></span>: Hankel at lag 1, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{i \times j} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8247em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8247em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">i</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.05724em;">j</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eigensystem_realization_algorithm">Eigensystem realization algorithm</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (NumPy &#x26; control):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from scipy.linalg import svd import control # Simulate response data (replace with real) p, m, T = 1, 1, 200 Y_all = np.random.rand(p*m, T) # (p*m, T) i, j = 10, 40 H0 = np.zeros((p*i, j)) for row in range(i): H0[row*p:(row+1)*p, :] = Y_all[:, row:row+j] U, s, Vh = svd(H0, full_matrices=False) r = 5 U_r, S_r, V_r = U[:, :r], np.diag(s[:r]), Vh.conj().T[:, :r] H1 = np.zeros((p*i, j)) for row in range(i): H1[row*p:(row+1)*p, :] = Y_all[:, row+1:row+1+j] S_inv_sqrt = np.diag(1/np.sqrt(s[:r])) A_hat = S_inv_sqrt @ (U_r.T @ H1 @ V_r) @ S_inv_sqrt C_hat = (U_r @ np.diag(np.sqrt(s[:r])))[:p, :] B_hat = (np.diag(np.sqrt(s[:r])) @ V_r.T)[:, :m] D_hat = Y_all[:,0].reshape(p, m) sys_era = control.ss(A_hat, B_hat, C_hat, D_hat) </code></pre> <h3>4. Non-linear ARX Neural Net (NARX)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Nonlinear dynamics with known delays; extends ARX for processes like distillation condensers.</li> </ul> class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1514em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">u</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: Lagged inputs count, integer.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonlinear_autoregressive_model">Nonlinear autoregressive model</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.optim as optim # Data prep data = np.loadtxt('distillation_data.csv', delimiter=',') u = data[:, 1:3] y = data[:, 3:5] ny, nu = 3, 2 def create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny): X, Y = [], [] for k in range(max(nu, ny), len(y)): lagged_y = y[k-ny:k][::-1].flatten() lagged_u = u[k-nu:k][::-1].flatten() X.append(np.concatenate([lagged_y, lagged_u])) Y.append(y[k]) return np.array(X), np.array(Y) X, Y = create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny) scaler_X = MinMaxScaler() scaler_Y = MinMaxScaler() X_scaled = scaler_X.fit_transform(X) Y_scaled = scaler_Y.fit_transform(Y) X_train, X_test, Y_train, Y_test = train_test_split(X_scaled, Y_scaled, test_size=0.2, shuffle=False) class NARXNet(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, hidden_dim=64): super().__init__() self.fc1 = nn.Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc2 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc3 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim) def forward(self, x): x = torch.relu(self.fc1(x)) x = torch.relu(self.fc2(x)) return self.fc3(x) input_dim = ny * y.shape[1] + nu * u.shape[1] model = NARXNet(input_dim, y.shape[1]) optimizer = optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001) criterion = nn.MSELoss() for epoch in range(200): outputs = model(torch.tensor(X_train, dtype=torch.float32)) loss = criterion(outputs, torch.tensor(Y_train, dtype=torch.float32)) optimizer.zero_grad() loss.backward() optimizer.step() # Validate with torch.no_grad(): y_pred = model(torch.tensor(X_test, dtype=torch.float32)).numpy() mse = np.mean((Y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>5. Recurrent Neural Net (LSTM)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Sequential data with long dependencies; predictive maintenance in IT/OT systems.</li> </ul> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2083em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2222em;"></span><span <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> h_t </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8444em;vertical-align:-0.15em;"></span><span mtight">t</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^h </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8491em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8491em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">h</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_short-term_memory">Long short-term memory</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.optim as optim # Data prep (example for condenser) data = np.loadtxt('condenser_data.csv', delimiter=',') u = data[:, 1:2] y = data[:, 5:6] seq_len = 10 def create_sequences(u, y, seq_len): X, Y = [], [] for t in range(seq_len, len(y)): seq_x = np.concatenate([u[t-seq_len:t], y[t-seq_len:t-1]], axis=1) X.append(seq_x) Y.append(y[t]) return np.array(X), np.array(Y) X, Y = create_sequences(u, y, seq_len) scaler_X = MinMaxScaler() scaler_Y = MinMaxScaler() X_scaled = scaler_X.fit_transform(X.reshape(-1, X.shape[-1])).reshape(X.shape) Y_scaled = scaler_Y.fit_transform(Y) X_train, X_test, Y_train, Y_test = train_test_split(X_scaled, Y_scaled, test_size=0.2, shuffle=False) class LSTMNet(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_dim, hidden_dim, output_dim, num_layers=1): super().__init__() self.hidden_dim = hidden_dim self.num_layers = num_layers self.lstm = nn.LSTM(input_dim, hidden_dim, num_layers, batch_first=True) self.fc = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim) def forward(self, x): h0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_dim).to(x.device) c0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_dim).to(x.device) out, _ = self.lstm(x, (h0, c0)) out = self.fc(out[:, -1, :]) return out input_dim = 2 # m + p-1 hidden_dim = 64 output_dim = 1 model = LSTMNet(input_dim, hidden_dim, output_dim) optimizer = optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001) criterion = nn.MSELoss() for epoch in range(200): outputs = model(torch.tensor(X_train, dtype=torch.float32)) loss = criterion(outputs, torch.tensor(Y_train, dtype=torch.float32)) optimizer.zero_grad() loss.backward() optimizer.step() # Validate with torch.no_grad(): y_pred = model(torch.tensor(X_test, dtype=torch.float32)).numpy() mse = np.mean((Y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>6. Gaussian Process Regression (GPR)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Uncertainty quantification in small-data regimes; pairs with MPC for robust manufacturing.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>∼</mo><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">G</mi><mi mathvariant="script">P</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mo mathvariant="normal" lspace="0em" rspace="0em">′</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> y(x) \sim \mathcal{GP}(m(x), k(x, x')) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord <pre><code class="language-python">from sklearn.gaussian_process import GaussianProcessRegressor from sklearn.gaussian_process.kernels import RBF, ConstantKernel kernel = ConstantKernel() * RBF() gpr = GaussianProcessRegressor(kernel=kernel) gpr.fit(X_train, y_train) y_pred, sigma = gpr.predict(X_test, return_std=True) mse = np.mean((y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>7. </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: State at k, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^n </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">n</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koopman_operator">Koopman operator</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (pykoopman):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">from pykoopman import Koopman from pykoopman.observables import Polynomial from pykoopman.regression import EDMD observables = Polynomial(degree=3) regressor = EDMD() model = Koopman(observables=observables, regressor=regressor) model.fit(x_train, x_next_train) K = model.A print("K shape:", K.shape) </code></pre> <h3>9. Model-Based Reinforcement Learning (PILCO, PETS)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Optimal control under uncertainty; bridges RL with models for adaptive manufacturing.</li> </ul> <p>PILCO: GPR dynamics, optimizes return via gradients.</p> <p>PETS: Neural ensembles for uncertainty, trajectory sampling.</p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>f: Dynamics model, Internal Model Control (IMC)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Processes with delays; robust to mismatches.</p> <p>Decompose <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1.0361em;vertical-align:-0.2861em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">G</span><span href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relay_autotuning">Relay autotuning</a></p> <h3>14. Genetic Algorithm (GA)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Optimizing non-differentiable objectives like hybrid tuning.</p> <p>Evolve {θ_i} via selection, crossover, mutation to minimize J(θ).</p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>J(θ): Objective, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord mathbb">R</span></span></span></span>.</li> <li>θ: Parameters, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>p</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^p </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">p</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> <li>{θ_i}: Population.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_algorithm">Genetic algorithm</a></p> <h3>15. Custom Algorithm Creator</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Bespoke IT/OT hybrids, e.g., subspace + neural residuals.</p> <p>Build via scikit-learn: <a href="https://scikit-learn.org/stable/developers/develop.html#rolling-your-own-estimator">scikit-learn custom estimator</a>.</p> <p><strong>Innovation</strong>: Augment with SINDy for physics-ML fusion; integrate plc-gbt for DV correlation.</p> <h2>Pros/Cons of Key Algorithms</h2> <table><thead><tr><th>Algorithm Group</th><th>Pros</th><th>Cons</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Linear ID (1-3)</td><td>Fast, interpretable, low data needs</td><td>Limited to linear dynamics</td></tr><tr><td>Nonlinear/ML (4-7)</td><td>Handles complexity, uncertainty</td><td>Data-hungry, less interpretable</td></tr><tr><td>RL/Advanced (8-10)</td><td>Adaptive to changes</td><td>Computationally intensive, safety risks</td></tr><tr><td>Classical Control (11-14)</td><td>Simple deployment</td><td>Poor for multivariable/disturbances</td></tr></tbody></table> <h2>State Estimation</h2> <p>For unmeasured states in subspace/MPC: Use Kalman filter <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>x</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>K</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><mi>C</mi><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>x</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \hat{x}_{k+1} = A \hat{x}_k + B u_k + K (y_k - C \hat{x}_k) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.9028em;vertical-align:-0.2083em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord accent"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6944em;"><span style="top:-3em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span></span><span style="top:-3em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="accent-body" style="left:-0.2222em;"><span class="mord">^</span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3361em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span><span class="mbin mtight">+</span><span class="mord mtight">1</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2083em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8444em;vertical-align:-0.15em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal">A</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord accent"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6944em;"><span style="top:-3em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span></span><span style="top:-3em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="accent-body" style="left:-0.2222em;"><span mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" Integrate for robustness.</p> <h2>Example</h2> <p><strong>GOAL</strong>: Create a Python-based recipe from process data to subspace model to MPC with explicit feedforward. Replace PID loops with one predictive, multivariable controller handling 6 DV disturbances (individually/combined) for resilient manufacturing.</p> <p><strong>Process Definition</strong>: Identify variables.</p> <ul> <li>State: x ∈ <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^n </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">n</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> (n chosen via subspace or physics).</li> <li>MV: u(k)</li> <li>DVs: d(k) = [d1,…,d6]^T</li> <li>PVs: y = [y1, y2, y3]^T, SP: r for y1.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Data Collection</strong>: Excite u and each di with PRBS or steps (independently/combined). Log y1, y2 at sampling Ts. Compute y3 = fdrv(y1, y2).</p> <p><strong>Model Identification</strong>: Use N4SID to estimate <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math One-step ahead: Compare \hat{y}(k|k-1) vs. y(k).</p> <p>Multi-step: Simulate open-loop over horizon N. Residuals: Ensure whiteness (e.g., autocorrelation test).</p> <p>Metrics: Fit Percentage (FIT) = 100 (1 - |y - \hat{y}|_2 / |y - \bar{y}|_2).</p> <p><strong>Python</strong>: from scipy.signal import lsim</p> <p>y_sim, _, _ = lsim((A, [B, E], C, 0), np.hstack((u, d)), t) # Simulate</p> <p><strong>MPC Formulation</strong>: At each k, solve Quadratic Program (QP): <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>min</mi><mo>⁡</mo></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><msubsup><mo mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>i</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><mi>r</mi><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>Q</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>R</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \min_{\{u_{k+i}\}_{i=0}^{N-1}} \sum_{i=1}^N \| y_{1,k+i|k} - r \|_Q^2 + \sum_{i=0}^{N-1} \| \Delta u_{k+i} \|_R^2 </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" Constraints: u_min ≤ u ≤ u_max, Δu_min ≤ Δu ≤ Δu_max. For robustness, add slack variables for soft constraints.</p> <p><strong>Python</strong> (cvxpy for offline testing):</p> <p>import cvxpy as cp</p> <p>u_opt = cp.Variable(N) # Horizon N</p> <h1>Define objective/constraints via matrices; solve with qpOASES for real-time</h1> <p>prob = cp.Problem(objective, constraints)</p> <p>prob.solve()</p> <p><strong>Feedforward Compensation</strong>: Individual: For each di, static gain Kff,i = - (C (I-A)^{-1} E[:,i]) / (C (I-A)^{-1} B) to cancel effect on y1.</p> <p>Combined: Kff ∈ <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 6} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8141em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8141em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">1</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mtight">6</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> from steady-state Gd = C (I-A)^{-1} E, Kff = (C (I-A)^{-1} B)^{-1} Gd. u_total = u_MPC + u_ff, u_ff = -Kff d(k). For dynamics, use model-based preview if d is predictable.</p> <p><strong>Offline Testing</strong>: Simulate closed-loop with real disturbance profiles. Compare vs. PID cascade/pure feedback MPC using metrics like Integral of Squared Error (ISE) (∫ e^2 dt).Innovation: Test hybrid (MPC + LSTM for residuals) for drift.</p> <p><strong>Deployment</strong>: Embed QP in real-time solver (e.g., qpOASES via Python wrapper or CVXGEN codegen).</p> <p>Run on edge PC/gateway; send u_total to Programmable Logic Controller (PLC).Update A,B,E recursively (e.g., Recursive Least Squares (RLS)): θk+1 = θk + Pkϕk (yk − ϕkTθk).</p> <p>Tip: Stream data to storage / analytics for periodic retraining, enabling adaptive control at scale.</p> <p>1.1.2: Why Truncate SVD:</p> <p>Here are the main reasons and rules of thumb for choosing to truncate:</p> <p>Noise Filtering / Denoising– In practical data there’s almost always measurement noise or unmodeled high-frequency components. The smallest singular values often correspond primarily to that noise.– By dropping all σₖ below some threshold (or below machine precision), you remove spurious components and get a cleaner, more robust approximation.</p> <p>Model Order Reduction– In system identification, you rarely want an A matrix of full rank = min(i,j). Instead you pick a reduced order r ≪ min(i,j) that balances fidelity vs. complexity.– Truncating to the r largest singular values gives you a rank-r Hankel approximation</p> <p>H(0)≈Ur Σr VrT H^{(0)} \approx U_r,\Sigma_r,V_r^TH(0)≈UrΣrVrT</p> <p>and leads to an n-state model (with n = r) that’s far easier to analyze and implement in an MPC or observer.</p> <p>Energy (Variance) Criterion– A common guideline is to choose r so that</p> <p>∑k=1rσk2∑k=1min⁡(i,j)σk2  ≥  α\frac{\sum_{k=1}^r \sigma_k^2}{\sum_{k=1}^{\min(i,j)} \sigma_k^2} ;\ge; \alpha∑k=1min(i,j)σk2∑k=1rσk2≥α</p> <p>for some energy level α (e.g.\ 95 % or 99 %). This ensures you’re retaining the vast majority of the data’s “power.”</p> <p>Singular‐Value Gap– Often there’s a clear “elbow” or big drop between σₙ and σₙ₊₁ when you plot them in descending order. That gap suggests a natural cut-off for the dominant modes vs. the rest.</p> <p>Computational Constraints– Keeping fewer modes makes subsequent computations (e.g.\ eigenvalue analyses, QP solves inside an MPC) dramatically faster and less memory-hungry—critical if you’re embedding the model on an edge device or PLC gateway.</p> <p>Cross-Validation / AIC / BIC– For a more statistically rigorous pick, you can treat r as a hyperparameter and choose it to minimize prediction error on held-out data, or via information-criteria like AIC/BIC that penalize complexity.</p> <p>In practice:</p> <p>If you see σ₁, …, σ₁₀ are each O(1) but σ₁₁, …, σ₂₀ drop to O(10⁻³), you’d likely truncate at r = 10.</p> <p>If you need a 5-state controller (because your PLC only has capacity for an n≤5 model), you truncate to the top 5 singular values even if the energy retained is, say, 90 %.</p> <p>If your ultimate goal is denoising and visualization, you might use an energy threshold (e.g. 99 %) to decide r.</p> <p>In all cases, truncation is the lever that trades off accuracy (keeping more σ’s) against simplicity and robustness (keeping fewer σ’s).</p> <p>Notes:</p> <p>Cross-Library Integration: Many algorithms require multiple libraries (e.g., SINDy uses pysindy with NumPy/SciPy for matrix operations, scikit-learn for LASSO).</p> <p>IT/OT Relevance: Libraries like cvxpy, qpOASES, and do-mpc support real-time control on edge devices, while PyTorch and GPy enable cloud-based training for adaptive models.</p> <p>Innovation: Combining pysindy (physics-informed) with PyTorch (neural residuals) can create hybrid models for manufacturing, aligning with the document’s “Custom Algorithm Creator” vision. </p> <p>Review the attached document, pay close attention to the Introduction section and ensure the main body of the document meets or exceeds the introduction requirements. If you plan to explain something, just explain it.</p> <p>Output only the revised document.<document filename="MPC-overview.md"></document></p> <h1>From PID to Predictive: A Hands-On Guide to Model Predictive Control in the Era of Industrial Digital Transformation</h1> <h2>Introduction: The Importance of MPC and Machine Learning Strategies</h2> <p>Industrial process control is undergoing a seismic shift as digital transformation and Industry 4.0+ bring information technology (IT) and operational technology (OT) closer together. Full convergence of these technologies is necessary to realize world-class optimization in manufacturing. This requires innovative solutions to address complex infrastructure (technical debt) amid pressures like greater product variety, shorter production runs, higher raw material utilization, stricter quality standards, sustainability goals, and environmental requirements—all while maximizing efficiency and uptime.</p> <p>Traditional control methods like PID can only take a facility so far. Machine learning (ML) and model predictive control (MPC) emerge as game-changers. These concepts are not new, but accessible tools now drive innovation.</p> <p>With ML and MPC acting as intelligent layers on existing automation systems, continuously optimizing multiple objectives (cost reduction, quality improvement) in real time for unprecedented efficiency. Advanced process control (APC) strategies like MPC are key enablers for Industry 4.0+—core capabilities for digital transformation. By leveraging data and models, they help industries remain competitive in connected, data-driven operations.</p> <h3>Foundations and Motivation</h3> <p>Traditional PID control provides stable regulation for single loops but struggles with multivariable interactions, delays, and constraints common in modern manufacturing. PID reacts to current errors without prediction, leading to suboptimal performance under disturbances (e.g., varying mash density affecting proofing).</p> <p>The industry pivots to model-based strategies like MPC, which use dynamic models to forecast behavior and optimize actions. In the Big Data era of Industry 4.0, ML enhances this by learning from vast datasets, enabling adaptive, proactive control. This convergence of PID fundamentals with MPC/ML hybrids bridges gaps, offering resilience and efficiency for sustainability and agility goals.</p> <h3>Modeling and MPC Design</h3> <p>Develop dynamic models via first principles (e.g., mass/energy balances) or data-driven methods (e.g., subspace ID). These models form MPC's core, predicting future states to minimize costs subject to constraints.</p> <p><strong>First-Principles Example</strong> (Heat Exchanger): <span class="katex"><span </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.345em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose nulldelimiter"></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1.5523em;vertical-align:-0.5423em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mopen nulldelimiter"></span><span class="mfrac"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:1.01em;"><span style="top:-2.655em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.22222em;">V</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">ρ</span><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.07153em;">C</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1645em;"><span style="top:-2.357em;margin-left:-0.0715em;margin-right:0.0714em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.5em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size3 size1 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">p</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2819em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span style="top:-3.23em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="frac-line" style="border-bottom-width:0.04em;"></span></span><span style="top:-3.485em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:3em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">F</span><span class="mopen mtight">(</span><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">T</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3281em;"><span style="top:-2.357em;margin-left:-0.1389em;margin-right:0.0714em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.5em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size3 size1 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">in</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.143em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mbin mtight">−</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">T</span><span class="mclose mtight">)</span><span class="mbin mtight">+</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.10903em;">U</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">A</span><span class="mopen mtight">(</span><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">T</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1645em;"><span style="top:-2.357em;margin-left:-0.1389em;margin-right:0.0714em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.5em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size3 size1 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">c</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.143em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mbin mtight">−</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.13889em;">T</span><span class="mclose mtight">)</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.5423em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose nulldelimiter"></span></span></span></span></span></p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>T: Temperature (°F).</li> <li>t: Time (s).</li> <li>F: Flow (gpm).</li> <li>T_in: Inlet temperature (°F).</li> <li>UA: Heat transfer coefficient (BTU/°F-s).</li> <li>T_c: Coolant temperature (°F).</li> <li>V: Volume (gal).</li> <li>ρ: Density (lbs/gal).</li> <li>C_p: Heat capacity (BTU/lb-°F).</li> </ul> <p><strong>Python (SciPy for Simulation)</strong>:</p> <pre><code class="language-python">from scipy.integrate import odeint import numpy as np def model(T, t, F, T_in, UA, T_c, V, rho, Cp): dTdt = (F * (T_in - T) + UA * (T_c - T)) / (V * rho * Cp) return dTdt params = (100, 200, 50, 100, 10, 8.345, 1.0) # Example values t = np.linspace(0, 100, 100) T0 = 150 T_sim = odeint(model, T0, t, args=params) </code></pre> <p>For MPC design, discretize and formulate QP (see example below).</p> <h3>Integration with PLC Control</h3> <p>Integrate MPC with PLCs by linking outputs to PIDE setpoints. Use OPC UA for data exchange; handle mode switching (e.g., from MPC to manual/PID) with bumpless transfer (smooth handover to avoid jumps). Ensure safety via watchdogs (fallback to PID on MPC failure).</p> <p><strong>Python (control for PID-MPC Hybrid)</strong>:</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import control import numpy as np # PID model Kp, Ti, Td = 1.0, 2.0, 0.5 pid = control.tf([Kp * Td, Kp, Kp / Ti], [1, 0]) # PD form approximation # MPC setpoint from simulation (placeholder) sp_mpc = np.sin(np.linspace(0, 10, 100)) * 50 + 150 # Simulate PID tracking MPC SP plant = control.tf(1, [2, 1]) # Example process closed = control.feedback(pid * plant) t, y = control.step_response(closed * control.tf(sp_mpc, 1), T=np.linspace(0, 10, 100)) # Plot y vs sp_mpc for handover check </code></pre> <p>For plc-gbt, use Python wrappers to read PLC tags and write MPC outputs.</p> <h3>Advanced Enhancements</h3> <p>Enhance control with adaptive ML like NARX for nonlinear modeling. Case study: NARX improves accuracy in distillation condensers by capturing delays/disturbances, serving as soft sensor for quality (e.g., proof prediction).</p> <p><strong>NARX Case Study</strong> (Python with PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.optim as optim # Data prep (example for condenser) data = np.loadtxt('condenser_data.csv', delimiter=',') u = data[:, 1:3] y = data[:, 3:5] ny, nu = 3, 2 def create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny): X, Y = [], [] for k in range(max(nu, ny), len(y)): lagged_y = y[k-ny:k][::-1].flatten() lagged_u = u[k-nu:k][::-1].flatten() X.append(np.concatenate([lagged_y, lagged_u])) Y.append(y[k]) return np.array(X), np.array(Y) X, Y = create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny) scaler_X = MinMaxScaler() scaler_Y = MinMaxScaler() X_scaled = scaler_X.fit_transform(X) Y_scaled = scaler_Y.fit_transform(Y) X_train, X_test, Y_train, Y_test = train_test_split(X_scaled, Y_scaled, test_size=0.2, shuffle=False) class NARXNet(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, hidden_dim=64): super().__init__() self.fc1 = nn.Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc2 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc3 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim) def forward(self, x): x = torch.relu(self.fc1(x)) x = torch.relu(self.fc2(x)) return self.fc3(x) input_dim = ny * y.shape[1] + nu * u.shape[1] model = NARXNet(input_dim, y.shape[1]) optimizer = optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001) criterion = nn.MSELoss() for epoch in range(200): outputs = model(torch.tensor(X_train, dtype=torch.float32)) loss = criterion(outputs, torch.tensor(Y_train, dtype=torch.float32)) optimizer.zero_grad() loss.backward() optimizer.step() # Validate with torch.no_grad(): y_pred = model(torch.tensor(X_test, dtype=torch.float32)).numpy() mse = np.mean((Y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) # Use as soft sensor: predict y from u, feed to MPC </code></pre> <p>Incorporate for adaptive control: Retrain NARX on plc-gbt data for evolving processes.</p> <h3>Best Practices and Troubleshooting</h3> <p>Tune MPC/PID via simulation (e.g., FIT >80%). Maintain fidelity with periodic re-identification. Diagnose issues: Monitor residuals (whiteness test), use plc-gbt for metrics (e.g., ISE). Fallbacks: Switch to PID on high variance. Best practices: Start small (single loop), scale to multivariable; validate hybrids offline.</p> <p><strong>Troubleshooting Table</strong>:</p> <table><thead><tr><th>Issue</th><th>Cause</th><th>Fix</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Oscillations</td><td>Model mismatch</td><td>Retune λ or retrain model</td></tr><tr><td>Constraint Violations</td><td>Unmodeled DVs</td><td>Add slack variables in QP</td></tr><tr><td>Slow Response</td><td>Conservative λ</td><td>Reduce λ, test stability</td></tr><tr><td>Overfitting</td><td>High model order</td><td>Truncate SVD (energy >95%)</td></tr></tbody></table> <h2>Algorithms for Process Control</h2> <p>Each entry includes "When to Use," equations, variables, and Python examples for hands-on learning.</p> <h3>1. AutoRegressive with eXogenous Inputs (ARX / ARMAX)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Simple linear processes with known inputs/outputs: Quick baselines in stable lines.</li> <li>Fast online identification: Track drifts via recursive least squares.</li> <li>Low computation: Suitable for PLCs.</li> <li>Noise modeling (ARMAX): Separate dynamics from fluctuations.</li> <li>Foundation for predictive control: Anticipates outputs.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: ARX: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1.0085em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.03588em;">q</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8141em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">−</span><span class="mord mtight">1</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: Backward-shift operator.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoregressive_exogenous_model">Autoregressive exogenous model</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (control &#x26; NumPy):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np import control # Simulate data (replace with real) n_a, n_b = 2, 2 a = [0.5, -0.3] b = [1.0, 0.5] u = np.random.rand(100) y = np.zeros(100) for k in range(max(n_a, n_b), 100): y[k] = -sum(a[i-1] * y[k-i] for i in range(1, n_a+1)) + sum(b[j-1] * u[k-j] for j in range(1, n_b+1)) + np.random.normal(0, 0.1) # Estimate ARX sys_arx = control.arx(np.vstack([u, y]).T, n_a, n_b) print("Estimated coefficients:", sys_arx.a, sys_arx.b) </code></pre> <h3>2. Subspace Identification (N4SID, MOESP)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Multivariable systems with interactions.</li> <li>Black-box modeling without priors.</li> <li>Noise robustness via SVD projection.</li> <li>Efficient for large data.</li> <li>Foundation for MPC/observers.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>A</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo separator="true">,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>D</mi><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> x_{k+1} = A x_k + B u_k, \quad y_k = C x_k + D u_k </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subspace_identification">Subspace identification</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (control):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np import control # Simulate data (replace with real) n, l, m = 4, 2, 2 sys = control.rss(n, l, m, tsamp=0.1) u = np.random.rand(100, m) y = control.forced_response(sys, U=u.T)[1].T data = control.iddata(y, u, tsamp=0.1) model_order = 4 sys_n4sid = control.n4sid(data, model_order) print("A matrix:", sys_n4sid.A) </code></pre> <h3>3. Eigensystem Realization Algorithm (ERA)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Reduced-order models from impulse/step data.</li> <li>Vibration-heavy manufacturing.</li> <li>Learning dominant patterns for predictive MPC.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{j \times r} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8247em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8247em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.05724em;">j</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.02778em;">r</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> H(1) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.08125em;">H</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">)</span></span></span></span>: Hankel at lag 1, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{i \times j} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8247em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8247em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">i</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.05724em;">j</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eigensystem_realization_algorithm">Eigensystem realization algorithm</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (NumPy &#x26; control):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from scipy.linalg import svd import control # Simulate response data (replace with real) p, m, T = 1, 1, 200 Y_all = np.random.rand(p*m, T) # (p*m, T) i, j = 10, 40 H0 = np.zeros((p*i, j)) for row in range(i): H0[row*p:(row+1)*p, :] = Y_all[:, row:row+j] U, s, Vh = svd(H0, full_matrices=False) r = 5 U_r, S_r, V_r = U[:, :r], np.diag(s[:r]), Vh.conj().T[:, :r] H1 = np.zeros((p*i, j)) for row in range(i): H1[row*p:(row+1)*p, :] = Y_all[:, row+1:row+1+j] S_inv_sqrt = np.diag(1/np.sqrt(s[:r])) A_hat = S_inv_sqrt @ (U_r.T @ H1 @ V_r) @ S_inv_sqrt C_hat = (U_r @ np.diag(np.sqrt(s[:r])))[:p, :] B_hat = (np.diag(np.sqrt(s[:r])) @ V_r.T)[:, :m] D_hat = Y_all[:,0].reshape(p, m) sys_era = control.ss(A_hat, B_hat, C_hat, D_hat) </code></pre> <h3>4. Non-linear ARX Neural Net (NARX)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Nonlinear dynamics with known delays; extends ARX for processes like distillation condensers.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>y</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo \dots, u(k-n_u) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1514em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>m</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^m </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">m</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>u</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> n_u </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.5806em;vertical-align:-0.15em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">n</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1514em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">u</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: Lagged inputs count, integer.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonlinear_autoregressive_model">Nonlinear autoregressive model</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.optim as optim # Data prep data = np.loadtxt('distillation_data.csv', delimiter=',') u = data[:, 1:3] y = data[:, 3:5] ny, nu = 3, 2 def create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny): X, Y = [], [] for k in range(max(nu, ny), len(y)): lagged_y = y[k-ny:k][::-1].flatten() lagged_u = u[k-nu:k][::-1].flatten() X.append(np.concatenate([lagged_y, lagged_u])) Y.append(y[k]) return np.array(X), np.array(Y) X, Y = create_lagged_data(u, y, nu, ny) scaler_X = MinMaxScaler() scaler_Y = MinMaxScaler() X_scaled = scaler_X.fit_transform(X) Y_scaled = scaler_Y.fit_transform(Y) X_train, X_test, Y_train, Y_test = train_test_split(X_scaled, Y_scaled, test_size=0.2, shuffle=False) class NARXNet(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, hidden_dim=64): super().__init__() self.fc1 = nn.Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc2 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim) self.fc3 = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim) def forward(self, x): x = torch.relu(self.fc1(x)) x = torch.relu(self.fc2(x)) return self.fc3(x) input_dim = ny * y.shape[1] + nu * u.shape[1] model = NARXNet(input_dim, y.shape[1]) optimizer = optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001) criterion = nn.MSELoss() for epoch in range(200): outputs = model(torch.tensor(X_train, dtype=torch.float32)) loss = criterion(outputs, torch.tensor(Y_train, dtype=torch.float32)) optimizer.zero_grad() loss.backward() optimizer.step() # Validate with torch.no_grad(): y_pred = model(torch.tensor(X_test, dtype=torch.float32)).numpy() mse = np.mean((Y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>5. Recurrent Neural Net (LSTM)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Sequential data with long dependencies; predictive maintenance in IT/OT systems.</li> </ul> </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8491em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8491em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">h</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_short-term_memory">Long short-term memory</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (PyTorch):</p> <pre><code class="language-python">import numpy as np from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.optim as optim # Data prep (example for condenser) data = np.loadtxt('condenser_data.csv', delimiter=',') u = data[:, 1:2] y = data[:, 5:6] seq_len = 10 def create_sequences(u, y, seq_len): X, Y = [], [] for t in range(seq_len, len(y)): seq_x = np.concatenate([u[t-seq_len:t], y[t-seq_len:t-1]], axis=1) X.append(seq_x) Y.append(y[t]) return np.array(X), np.array(Y) X, Y = create_sequences(u, y, seq_len) scaler_X = MinMaxScaler() scaler_Y = MinMaxScaler() X_scaled = scaler_X.fit_transform(X.reshape(-1, X.shape[-1])).reshape(X.shape) Y_scaled = scaler_Y.fit_transform(Y) X_train, X_test, Y_train, Y_test = train_test_split(X_scaled, Y_scaled, test_size=0.2, shuffle=False) class LSTMNet(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_dim, hidden_dim, output_dim, num_layers=1): super().__init__() self.hidden_dim = hidden_dim self.num_layers = num_layers self.lstm = nn.LSTM(input_dim, hidden_dim, num_layers, batch_first=True) self.fc = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim) def forward(self, x): h0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_dim).to(x.device) c0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_dim).to(x.device) out, _ = self.lstm(x, (h0, c0)) out = self.fc(out[:, -1, :]) return out input_dim = 2 # m + p-1 hidden_dim = 64 output_dim = 1 model = LSTMNet(input_dim, hidden_dim, output_dim) optimizer = optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001) criterion = nn.MSELoss() for epoch in range(200): outputs = model(torch.tensor(X_train, dtype=torch.float32)) loss = criterion(outputs, torch.tensor(Y_train, dtype=torch.float32)) optimizer.zero_grad() loss.backward() optimizer.step() # Validate with torch.no_grad(): y_pred = model(torch.tensor(X_test, dtype=torch.float32)).numpy() mse = np.mean((Y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>6. Gaussian Process Regression (GPR)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Uncertainty quantification in small-data regimes; pairs with MPC for robust manufacturing.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Equations</strong>: <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>∼</mo><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">G</mi><mi mathvariant="script">P</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mo mathvariant="normal" lspace="0em" rspace="0em">′</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> y(x) \sim \mathcal{GP}(m(x), k(x, x')) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.03588em;">y</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="mclose">)</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">∼</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1.0019em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathcal" style="margin-right:0.0593em;">G</span><span class="mord mathcal" style="margin-right:0.08222em;">P</span></span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal">m</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="mclose">)</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.1667em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.1667em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.7519em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">′</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose">))</span></span></span></span></p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> y(x) </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.03588em;">y</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal">x</span><span class="mclose">)</span></span></span></span>: Output, in <span = GaussianProcessRegressor(kernel=kernel) gpr.fit(X_train, y_train) y_pred, sigma = gpr.predict(X_test, return_std=True) mse = np.mean((y_test - y_pred)**2) print("MSE:", mse) </code></pre> <h3>7. print("K shape:", K.shape) </code></pre> <h3>9. Model-Based Reinforcement Learning (PILCO, PETS)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>Optimal control under uncertainty; bridges RL with models for adaptive manufacturing.</li> </ul> <p>PILCO: GPR dynamics, optimizes return via gradients.</p> <p>PETS: Neural ensembles for uncertainty, trajectory sampling.</p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>f: Dynamics model, unitless.</li> <li><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mrow><annotation href="https://mlg.eng.cam.ac.uk/pilco/">PILCO</a> <strong>Ref (PETS)</strong>: <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.12114">arXiv:1805.12114</a></p> <p><strong>Python Implementation</strong> (GPy for PILCO, PyTorch for PETS): See expanded snippets in body for full code.</p> <h3>10. Policy-Gradient RL (DDPG, TD3, SAC)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Continuous-action spaces in uncertain environments; model-free for direct policy learning. For this example, we will use distillate proofing control as our example: A highly uncertain process where alcohol concentration (proof) must be maintained (e.g., 100 -160 proof SP) – Low Wine Average setpoint = 130 proof and High Wine Average setpoint = 140 Amid many potential disturbance variables causing proof disturbances like varying beerfeed composition (total mash density, %ABV, recipe differences, etc.), still temperature drifts due to varying steam supply pressure, column pressure fluctuations, column beer bottom level, and others which cause risk of producing off-spec product or energy waste. DDPG/TD3/SAC can learn deterministic or stochastic policies to adjust MVs (e.g., reflux ratios or steam inputs) for optimal proof tracking, incorporating exploration for safe adaptation. The deadtime and lag between Column vapor temperature and vapor condensing back to liquid and running thru the proofing meter has a very long and dependent on its own set of disturbance variables such as reflux rate, cooling water flow and temperature, vapor concentration, ect.</p> <p><strong>Equations</strong> (DDPG/TD3): Actor <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>μ</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>s</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><msup><mi>θ</mi><mi>μ</mi></msup><mo href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relay_autotuning">Relay autotuning</a></p> <h3>14. Genetic Algorithm (GA)</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Optimizing non-differentiable objectives like hybrid tuning.</p> <p>Evolve {θ_i} via selection, crossover, mutation to minimize J(θ).</p> <p><strong>Variables</strong>:</p> <ul> <li>J(θ): Objective, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord mathbb">R</span></span></span></span>.</li> <li>θ: Parameters, in <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>p</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^p </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">p</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li> <li>{θ_i}: Population.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Ref</strong>: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_algorithm">Genetic algorithm</a></p> <h3>15. Custom Algorithm Creator</h3> <p><strong>When to Use</strong>: Bespoke IT/OT hybrids, e.g., subspace + neural residuals.</p> <p>Build via scikit-learn: <a href="https://scikit-learn.org/stable/developers/develop.html#rolling-your-own-estimator">scikit-learn custom estimator</a>.</p> <p><strong>Innovation</strong>: Augment with SINDy for physics-ML fusion; integrate plc-gbt for DV correlation.</p> <h2>Pros/Cons of Key Algorithms</h2> <table><thead><tr><th>Algorithm Group</th><th>Pros</th><th>Cons</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Linear ID (1-3)</td><td>Fast, interpretable, low data needs</td><td>Limited to linear dynamics</td></tr><tr><td>Nonlinear/ML (4-7)</td><td>Handles complexity, uncertainty</td><td>Data-hungry, less interpretable</td></tr><tr><td>RL/Advanced (8-10)</td><td>Adaptive to changes</td><td>Computationally intensive, safety risks</td></tr><tr><td>Classical Control (11-14)</td><td>Simple deployment</td><td>Poor for multivariable/disturbances</td></tr></tbody></table> <h2>State Estimation</h2> <p>For unmeasured states in subspace/MPC: Use Kalman filter <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.07153em;">C</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord accent"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6944em;"><span mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight" style="margin-right:0.03148em;">k</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s"> </span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" Integrate for robustness.</p> <h2>Example</h2> <p><strong>GOAL</strong>: Create a Python-based recipe from process data to subspace model to MPC with explicit feedforward. Replace PID loops with one predictive, multivariable controller handling 6 DV disturbances (individually/combined) for resilient manufacturing.</p> <p><strong>Process Definition</strong>: Identify variables.</p> <ul> <li>State: x ∈ <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^n </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.6889em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">n</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> (n chosen via subspace or physics).</li> <li>MV: u(k)</li> <li>DVs: d(k) = [d1,…,d6]^T</li> <li>PVs: y = [y1, y2, y3]^T, SP: r for y1.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Data Collection</strong>: Excite u and each di with PRBS or steps (independently/combined). Log y1, y2 at sampling Ts. Metrics: FIT = 100 (1 - |y - \hat{y}|_2 / |y - \bar{y}|_2).</p> <p><strong>Python</strong>: from scipy.signal import lsim</p> <p>y_sim, _, _ = lsim((A, [B, E], C, 0), np.hstack((u, d)), t) # Constraints: u_min ≤ u ≤ u_max, Δu_min ≤ Δu ≤ Δu_max. For robustness, add slack variables for soft constraints.</p> <p><strong>Python</strong> (cvxpy for offline testing):</p> <p>import cvxpy as cp</p> <p>u_opt = cp.Variable(N) # Horizon N</p> <h1>Define objective/constraints via matrices; solve with qpOASES for real-time</h1> <p>prob = cp.Problem(objective, constraints)</p> <p>prob.solve()</p> <p><strong>Feedforward Compensation</strong>: Individual: For each di, static gain Kff,i = - (C (I-A)^{-1} E[:,i]) / (C (I-A)^{-1} B) to cancel effect on y1.</p> <p>Combined: Kff ∈ <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex"> \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 6} </annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8141em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathbb">R</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.8141em;"><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">1</span><span class="mbin mtight">×</span><span class="mord mtight">6</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> from steady-state Gd = C (I-A)^{-1} E, Kff = (C (I-A)^{-1} B)^{-1} Gd. u_total = u_MPC + u_ff, u_ff = -Kff d(k). For dynamics, use model-based preview if d is predictable.</p> <p><strong>Offline Testing</strong>: Simulate closed-loop with real disturbance profiles. Compare vs. PID cascade/pure feedback MPC using metrics like ISE (∫ e^2 dt). Innovation: Test hybrid (MPC + LSTM for residuals) for drift.</p> <p><strong>Deployment</strong>: Embed QP in real-time solver (e.g., qpOASES via Python wrapper or CVXGEN codegen). Run on edge PC/gateway; send u_total to PLC. Update A,B,E via RLS: θ_{k+1} = θ_k + P_k ϕ_k (y_k - ϕ_k^T θ_k). Tip: Stream data to cloud for periodic retraining, enabling adaptive control at scale.</p> <p>1.1.2: Why Truncate SVD:</p> <p>Noise Filtering / Denoising: Drop small σ_k to remove noise.</p> <p>Model
